<a id="malaysia-migration-guide"></a>

# Malaysia: migration guide

Sources checked up to 23 Nov 2026.

How to go to Malaysia on a Bangladeshi passport, visa by visa, step by step. A raised number such as 3 is an official source; N3 is a news, NGO, research or guide source, listed under "Needs an official source"; a year, like N3 (2022), marks a source older than 2025. "Need to know" marks a point no source has yet confirmed. "You'll need:" lists what to have before starting a procedure.

Tk is the Bangladeshi taka (1 lakh is Tk 100,000); RM is the Malaysian ringgit, converted throughout at RM1 = about Tk 30.6, the rate on 6 November 2026 <sup>[N1](#n1)</sup>. BMET (Bureau of Manpower, Employment and Training) is the Bangladesh government agency that registers people who want to work abroad and gives them emigration clearance <sup>[1](#o1), [2](#o2)</sup>. IMI (Jabatan Imigresen Malaysia, the Immigration Department of Malaysia) approves work visas and runs the eVISA (electronic visa) system on its MyVISA website, malaysiavisa.imi.gov.my <sup>[3](#o3), [4](#o4), [5](#o5)</sup>. Bangladeshis need a visa to enter Malaysia for any purpose, even a short visit <sup>[3](#o3), [6](#o6)</sup>.

<a id="background"></a>

## 1 Background

<a id="in-one-minute"></a>

### 1.1 In one minute

**Malaysia is reopening to new Bangladeshi workers after being shut since 31 May 2024. Recruitment through the agencies on Malaysia's list was announced on 15 October 2026, medical tests began at 51 approved centres by 19 November, and the minister expected the first workers to go by the end of November, with BOESL's first flights due from the first week of December** <sup>[N2 (2024)](#n2), [7](#o7), [N3](#n3), [N4](#n4), [N5](#n5)</sup>. On 15 November the minister said the cost had been fixed at Tk 1,54,000 <sup>[N4](#n4)</sup>. BOESL, the government's recruiting agency, ran its own call: a 5 November circular for 112 men at DHL, with applications taken on OEP (the Overseas Employment Platform, BMET's job-seeker website) from 6 to 8 November and the shortlist drawn by lottery ([Step 1, item 3: Apply for government (BOESL) jobs](#step-1-find-a-real-job-item-3)) <sup>[8](#o8), [N5](#n5)</sup>. BOESL and the minister say that job is zero-cost, but what zero cost covers is disputed (see [Status](#status)) <sup>[8](#o8), [N6](#n6)</sup>.

A Malaysia job starts as a quota: permission for one Malaysian employer to bring in a set number of foreign workers <sup>[9](#o9)</sup>. Each slot becomes a "calling visa" (কলিং ভিসা) that is resold down a chain of agencies and brokers to the village, and every link takes a cut (see [How a job reaches you](#how-a-job-reaches-you-and-who-takes-a-cut), and who takes a cut) <sup>[N7](#n7), [N8](#n8), [N9 (2024)](#n9), [N10 (2018)](#n10)</sup>. The legal cap was Tk 78,990, but in 2022-24 workers paid about Tk 4.5 to 6 lakh, nearly all of it borrowed <sup>[10 (2022)](#o10), [N11](#n11), [N12](#n12), [N13 (2024)](#n13)</sup>. The job pays the RM1,700 minimum (about Tk 52,000) plus overtime, and the debt takes one to three years to clear, longer if the job or the pay doesn't come <sup>[11 (2024)](#o11), [N1](#n1), [N14 (2023)](#n14), [N9 (2024)](#n9)</sup>. The work pass ties you to one employer, and a second medical in Malaysia decides whether you get it at all <sup>[5](#o5)</sup>. The corridor has shut three times, each after a surge of departures and allegations against the agencies; the 2024 shutdown stranded 17,777 workers who had paid <sup>[N2 (2024)](#n2), [N15 (2018)](#n15), [N16 (2024)](#n16), [N17](#n17)</sup>. Under the restart, the medical checks set for 31 October 2026 did not start until Malaysia had vetted the centres <sup>[12](#o12), [N18](#n18), [N3](#n3)</sup>.

<a id="is-it-open-now-and-who-can-go"></a>

### 1.2 Is it open now, and who can go?

**Reopening. Closed to new workers since 31 May 2024, recruitment through the agencies on Malaysia's list was announced on 15 October 2026; medical tests had started by 19 November at 51 centres Malaysia approved, and the minister said on 15 November that workers should be able to go from the end of the following week to the end of November** <sup>[N2 (2024)](#n2), [7](#o7), [N3](#n3), [N4](#n4)</sup>. Before the tests started, the Ministry told people not to pay anyone, do a medical or hand over a passport until it issued formal instructions, and a medical done before the official start of 31 October does not count ([Step 2: Medical check in Bangladesh (Bio-Medical), once your VDR is approved](#step-2-medical-check-in-bangladesh-bio-medical-once-your-vdr-is-approved)) <sup>[N19](#n19), [12](#o12)</sup>.

**Who can go:** Immigration sets three conditions <sup>[5](#o5)</sup>:

- aged 18 to 45 when the employer applies;
- passed fit at a medical centre in Bangladesh;
- a job in one of the five allowed sectors (manufacturing, construction, plantation, agriculture, services).

No agreement covers recruiting Bangladeshi women, and the High Commission tells women not to go for general work <sup>[N20](#n20), [9](#o9)</sup>. Which routes are legal, and for whom: see [The channels](#the-channels). Extra tests and narrower conditions: see [Skill tests and narrower job orders](#skill-tests-and-narrower-job-orders).

> **Need to know**
> - Do any Bangladeshi women hold Malaysian factory work passes, and through which agencies did they go?

<a id="status"></a>

#### 1.2.1 Status

As of 19 November 2026 <sup>[7](#o7), [N21](#n21), [N3](#n3), [N4](#n4), [N5](#n5)</sup>:

- **Agencies:** Malaysia agreed to list 338 Bangladeshi agencies: BOESL (Bangladesh Overseas Employment and Services Ltd, the government-owned recruiting agency), 25 processing agencies and 312 recruiting agencies; under the Bangladesh-Malaysia MoU (memorandum of understanding), choosing and listing the agencies is the job of Malaysia's human resources ministry (see [The processing agency: the toll gate](#the-processing-agency-the-toll-gate); see [Who controls it](#who-controls-it)) <sup>[7](#o7), [N22](#n22)</sup>.
- **Medical tests:** started by 19 November at the 51 centres Malaysia approved ([Step 2: Medical check in Bangladesh (Bio-Medical), once your VDR is approved](#step-2-medical-check-in-bangladesh-bio-medical-once-your-vdr-is-approved)) <sup>[N3](#n3)</sup>. On 4 November the expatriates' welfare minister, Ariful Haque Chowdhury, had said centres already testing Malaysia-bound workers were doing so illegally and would face action <sup>[N18](#n18)</sup>.
- **Cost:** the minister told parliament on 15 November 2026 that the cost of sending a worker had been cut and fixed at Tk 1,54,000, and that strict action would be taken against anyone who collects more (see [The cap](#the-cap)) <sup>[N4](#n4)</sup>. On 5 November he had said the private-agency cost could be Tk 2.5 to 3 lakh, the range most agencies proposed, and no cost for private agencies was then set <sup>[N23](#n23), [N6](#n6), [N24](#n24)</sup>.
- **First flights:** the minister said on 15 November that workers should be able to go from the end of the following week to the end of November; BOESL's managing director, Mohammad Rashedul Haque, said on 8 November that flights for the selected DHL workers would start from the first week of December <sup>[N4](#n4), [N5](#n5)</sup>. On 5 November the minister had hoped the first zero-cost flights would leave in December <sup>[N6](#n6)</sup>.

> **Need to know**
> - Does the Tk 1,54,000 the minister announced on 15 November 2026 <sup>[N4](#n4)</sup> apply to workers sent by private agencies, or only to BOESL's workers, and does it replace the Tk 2.5 to 3 lakh he mentioned on 5 November <sup>[N23](#n23)</sup>?
> - Did Malaysia allow 337 Bangladeshi agencies <sup>[N21](#n21)</sup> or 338 <sup>[7](#o7)</sup>?
> - When did the first worker fly under the 2026 restart?

Some workers still go outside the restart (see [Size](#size)) <sup>[13](#o13), [N25](#n25)</sup>.

**The zero-cost places.** Malaysia has agreed in principle to take 10,000 workers at zero cost, the first through BOESL <sup>[7](#o7), [14](#o14)</sup>. The minister says these workers will pay nothing and be picked by online lottery from applicants registered on OEP, through BOESL circulars such as its 5 November call for 112 men at DHL Supply Chain (Malaysia), which took applications from 6 to 8 November ([Step 1, item 1: Register on the Overseas Employment Platform (OEP)](#step-1-find-a-real-job-item-1); [Step 1, item 3: Apply for government (BOESL) jobs](#step-1-find-a-real-job-item-3)) <sup>[N6](#n6), [8](#o8)</sup>. BOESL's managing director said this was the first time a lottery had been used to choose workers for government-channel sending to Malaysia <sup>[N5](#n5)</sup>. He also said on 8 November that BOESL had received demand letters for 558 more workers in Malaysia's tourism, manufacturing and caregiving sectors, with the employers bearing all costs including the medical and airfare, so these workers too would go with no migration cost <sup>[N5](#n5)</sup>. Prime Minister Tarique Rahman told parliament on 18 November 2026 that 10,000 people could be sent at almost no cost, and that 1,000 of them were to come from the families of victims of enforced disappearance and killing under the previous government and of those killed or injured in the 2024 movement <sup>[N26](#n26)</sup>. Dr Selim Reza of North South University's Centre for Migration Studies wrote in Prothom Alo on 4 September 2026 that, by the government's account, the first 10,000 are to fly through BOESL on special chartered flights free of every cost including airfare, that more are to follow, and that various statements have mentioned about two lakh workers <sup>[N27](#n27)</sup>. Anyone selling a "zero-cost" or "BOESL" place is selling something the government says is free ([Step 1, item 3: Apply for government (BOESL) jobs](#step-1-find-a-real-job-item-3)) <sup>[N6](#n6), [15](#o15)</sup>. Selim Reza wrote that a claim that paying money will get a name onto the list of 10,000 should be treated as likely fraud; that unless who may apply, the qualifications needed, how people are chosen and where to apply are published, the scheme could become a new line of business for brokers; and that the Ministry's warning not to hand over money, a medical test or a passport until government instructions come must be enforced <sup>[N27](#n27)</sup>.

The government contradicts itself on what "zero cost" means. The Ministry says the 10,000 go free of all migration costs including airfare, and the minister says not a single taka <sup>[14](#o14), [N6](#n6)</sup>. The state minister, Nurul Haque Nur, said in late October that they would still pay the airfare, the medical and a small BOESL service charge, and put the cost at Tk 1.55 to 1.60 lakh a worker <sup>[N28](#n28), [N24](#n24)</sup>. The Prime Minister said "almost no cost" <sup>[N26](#n26)</sup>. The DHL circular itself says zero-cost <sup>[8](#o8)</sup>.

> **Need to know**
> - Do zero-cost workers still pay the airfare, the medical and a small BOESL service charge, as the state minister said in late October <sup>[N28](#n28)</sup>?

**The agreement.** Employers have begun filing demand letters (চাহিদাপত্র, job orders) through FWCMS (Foreign Workers Centralised Management System, Malaysia's online visa system) <sup>[N28](#n28)</sup>. The Bangladesh-Malaysia MoU runs to 31 December 2026; on 5 November 2026 the minister said a new one would be signed on 31 December and wants every licensed agency allowed in <sup>[14](#o14), [N23](#n23)</sup>. Prothom Alo reported in April 2025 that the MoU was valid until August 2026, that agency selection would stay in Malaysia's hands if sending started within that period, and that the clause could be amended by agreement at renewal <sup>[N29](#n29)</sup>.

> **Need to know**
> - Was the MoU signed on 19 December 2021 <sup>[16 (2021)](#o16)</sup> or 18 December 2022 <sup>[N22](#n22)</sup>, and does it expire on 31 December 2026 <sup>[14](#o14)</sup>, or did it expire in December 2025 <sup>[N17](#n17)</sup> or August 2026 <sup>[N29](#n29)</sup>? Under which terms are the 2026 workers going?

<a id="how-the-restart-came-about"></a>

#### 1.2.2 How the restart came about

Ministers and government advisers announced or forecast a reopening several times from 2024 before the restart of 15 October 2026 (where it stands: see [Status](#status)) <sup>[N30](#n30), [N31](#n31), [N32](#n32), [N33](#n33), [7](#o7)</sup>.

**2024 to March 2026:**

- **31 May 2024:** Malaysia's last day for new workers to enter; the corridor has been shut since (why: see [What closed it before](#what-closed-it-before)) <sup>[N2 (2024)](#n2)</sup>.
- **5 October 2024:** Asif Nazrul, the expatriates' welfare adviser, said Bangladesh had made three proposals to Malaysia's Prime Minister Anwar Ibrahim: reopen the closed market, open recruitment to all agencies instead of a few, and consider raising Bangladeshi workers' wages <sup>[N34 (2024)](#n34)</sup>.
- **1 December 2024:** former ambassador M Humayun Kabir, president of the Bangladesh Enterprise Institute, said in an interview that the market had closed and had reopened on the initiative of the Chief Adviser, Muhammad Yunus <sup>[N35 (2024)](#n35)</sup>.
- **December 2024:** the expatriates' welfare secretary, Md Ruhul Amin, told Prothom Alo that the two countries would meet soon to reopen the market, closed since June 2024 <sup>[N36 (2024)](#n36)</sup>.
- **24 April 2025:** at a human chain outside Probashi Kallyan Bhaban, Eskaton, Dhaka, general members of BAIRA (Bangladesh Association of International Recruiting Agencies) said 13 countries had accepted Malaysia's conditions and kept sending workers while Bangladesh lagged behind <sup>[N37](#n37)</sup>.
- **13 to 15 May 2025:** a three-member delegation led by Asif Nazrul, with senior secretary Neyamat Ullah Bhuiyan and deputy secretary Md Sarwar Alam, went to Malaysia on 13 May to meet its human resources and home ministers; Ministry officials said Bangladesh would propose at the joint technical committee that all legal agencies be allowed to send workers, and that since the MoU ran to December 2025 there was no scope for a new agreement, only for amending the existing one <sup>[N17](#n17)</sup>. Bangladesh Civil Society for Migrants (BCSM), a platform of 23 organisations, recommended to the delegation that Malaysia be persuaded to take Bangladeshi workers by the same process as from its 14 other source countries, that cost be cut and a job on arrival guaranteed, and that a letter be sent to Malaysia's Prime Minister to drop one clause of the MoU <sup>[N38 (2024)](#n38), [N39](#n39)</sup>. After meeting Malaysia's home, human resources and trade ministers on 15 May, Asif Nazrul said in a Facebook video that Malaysia might take 1 to 1.5 lakh foreign workers in the coming months, that the human resources minister, Steven Sim Chee Keong, had assured Bangladesh top priority, and that Bangladesh had asked that all its agencies be allowed to send workers, which Malaysia said it would consider <sup>[N40](#n40)</sup>.
- **21 May 2025:** opening the two countries' joint working group meeting at the InterContinental hotel, Dhaka, the Chief Adviser's special envoy Lutfey Siddiqi said Malaysia wanted to take workers through a limited number of agencies because Bangladesh has so many, that amending the MoU would be discussed if needed for transparency, and that Malaysia had set no conditions, including withdrawal of the cases against those who formed the syndicate <sup>[N41](#n41)</sup>.
- **24 June 2025:** in a rejoinder to Prothom Alo, the Ministry said several joint working group and high-level meetings had been held, that it hoped the market would reopen soon by a transparent, broker-free method, and that a proposal for a joint, technology-based recruitment process was at its final stage <sup>[N42](#n42)</sup>.
- **August 2025:** the Chief Adviser was to visit Malaysia for three days from 11 August and meet Anwar Ibrahim in Putrajaya on 12 August; a senior foreign ministry official said removing Bangladeshis' problems in Malaysia's labour market and taking more workers would get priority; five MoUs on other subjects might be signed <sup>[N43](#n43)</sup>.
- **20 August 2025:** the Ministry's first-year report said a process had been started to open the market to new workers after top-level bilateral talks, and Asif Nazrul wrote in Prothom Alo that sending would start within about a month <sup>[N44](#n44), [N30](#n30)</sup>.
- **14 December 2025:** Prothom Alo reported that the market had later reopened on various conditions <sup>[N45](#n45)</sup>.
- **14 March 2026:** Prothom Alo reported that the market had been closed since June 2024 over allegations of syndicate trading and could not be reopened under the interim government (the government led by the Chief Adviser) despite its efforts <sup>[N46](#n46)</sup>.

> **Need to know**
> - Was any recruitment of new Bangladeshi workers open between June 2024 and October 2026, as reported on 1 December 2024 <sup>[N35 (2024)](#n35)</sup> and 14 December 2025 <sup>[N45](#n45)</sup>, or was it limited to the stranded 2024 workers and CLAB's construction intake <sup>[17](#o17), [N25](#n25)</sup>?

**From March 2026:**

- **24 March 2026:** the state minister for expatriates' welfare, Md Nurul Haque Nur, said government-level talks were under way to open the Malaysian and Vietnamese markets quickly <sup>[N47](#n47)</sup>.
- **5 April 2026:** the minister, Ariful Haque Chowdhury, told parliament that the government was not interested in sending workers through a syndicate of a limited number of agencies as before, that it was trying to keep the market open to all eligible licensed agencies, and that it was corresponding with Malaysia through the High Commission <sup>[N48](#n48)</sup>.
- **8 to 11 April 2026:** the minister and the adviser in charge of the Ministry visited Malaysia <sup>[N49](#n49)</sup>. At a bilateral meeting in Putrajaya on 9 April, led by Malaysia's human resources minister, Ramanan Ramakrishnan, and Ariful Haque Chowdhury, the two sides agreed to speed up reopening based on Malaysia's sector-wise demand, with a fair, ethical and transparent recruitment process, the Prime Minister's adviser Mahdi Amin said <sup>[N50](#n50)</sup>. Their joint press statement said steps would be taken to cut migration cost by reducing intermediaries, to use credible agencies, and to complete quickly the recruitment of workers who are stuck <sup>[N50](#n50)</sup>. On 12 April Mahdi Amin said reopening was a procedural matter, gave no date, and said Bangladesh had asked Malaysia to make the process syndicate-free; the same day the minister said in Sylhet "we have signed a joint venture, it is in our favour", without saying what was signed <sup>[N51](#n51), [N52](#n52)</sup>. Prime Minister Tarique Rahman told parliament on 15 April that the government was working to open the market <sup>[N49](#n49)</sup>.
- **10 June 2026:** in parliament the home minister, Salahuddin Ahmed, said the Prime Minister's first visit abroad had been fixed for Malaysia, to open its labour market; the state minister, Nurul Haque, said the market opens and closes again, that MPs wanted no syndicate there, and that the Prime Minister had given strict instructions that the government must not be given a bad name <sup>[N53](#n53)</sup>.
- **21 and 22 June 2026:** Tarique Rahman's first foreign visit was two days in Kuala Lumpur, with reopening the labour market a key priority, Prothom Alo reported from Malaysia's The Edge <sup>[N54](#n54)</sup>. At the talks in Putrajaya on 22 June, reopening the market, regularising Bangladeshis who had become irregular and renewing work permits were among the issues discussed, but the only documents signed or exchanged were a culture MoU and documents on counter-terrorism research and on investment <sup>[N55](#n55), [N56](#n56)</sup>. Tarique Rahman said he had asked Anwar Ibrahim to consider hiring more Bangladeshi workers and opening the market quickly, and that the two agreed recruitment must be transparent, fair and affordable so that middlemen's role shrinks and workers' costs fall; Mahdi Amin said he had asked for jobs for more workers at low cost through a transparent process, and that a 33-point joint statement on nine areas was issued <sup>[N57](#n57), [N58](#n58)</sup>. The two leaders agreed to form a joint working group (JWG) to review the existing labour MoU, and the joint statement said a JWG meeting would be called so that a new MoU on recruitment could be drafted from it; Selim Reza called this the most significant outcome <sup>[N59](#n59), [N60](#n60), [N61](#n61)</sup>. Malaysia's human resources minister, R Ramanan, said in a Facebook statement that his ministry would keep strengthening governance so that recruiting Bangladeshi workers is transparent, fair, ethical and free of exploitation <sup>[N59](#n59)</sup>. A Prothom Alo editorial of 22 July said the market was reopening after these talks <sup>[N62](#n62)</sup>.
- **23 and 24 June 2026:** the minister told parliament the government hoped the way to send workers would be cleared very soon and was trying to legalise Bangladeshis staying in Malaysia irregularly or in difficulty; the next day, at Osmani International Airport, Sylhet, he said good news could come within July and that Malaysia's human resources and home ministers would visit Bangladesh within July <sup>[N63](#n63), [N64](#n64)</sup>.
- **7 July 2026:** the minister told journalists at Sylhet Circuit House that Malaysia's market had reopened for Bangladesh from Monday 6 July as a result of the Prime Minister's visit; he gave no terms, quota or costs <sup>[N31](#n31)</sup>. Two days later he told parliament that a Malaysian delegation would visit in July and that he hoped the way to send workers would open very soon <sup>[N65](#n65)</sup>.
- **30 July 2026:** at the High Commission in Kuala Lumpur, Mahdi Amin said the two countries had agreed in principle to reopen the market at once, with work open to Bangladeshi workers in all sectors, and had set a target for workers to start arriving from the last week of August 2026 <sup>[N32](#n32)</sup>.
- **18 August 2026:** in Putrajaya, Malaysia's deputy tourism minister, Chiew Choon Man, and Bangladesh's state minister for civil aviation and tourism, M Rashiduzzaman Millat, discussed recruiting more qualified Bangladeshi workers for Malaysia's tourism and hospitality sector, with easier visa processing and pre-verification; no decision was reported <sup>[N66](#n66)</sup>.
- **21 August 2026:** Malaysia published the names of 25 Bangladeshi recruiting agencies on FWCMS (see [Who controls it](#who-controls-it)) <sup>[N67](#n67)</sup>.
- **30 August 2026:** after Malaysia's High Commissioner in Dhaka, Mohammad Suhadha bin Osman, called on the local government minister, the state minister for local government, Mir Shahe Alam, said Malaysia would start taking two lakh workers towards the end of September through listed companies, plus another 10,000 entirely free of cost, and that the process would be completed within six months <sup>[N33](#n33)</sup>.
- **3 and 16 September 2026:** the minister told parliament the way was expected to open very soon; the expatriates' welfare secretary, Md Mokhtar Ahmed, said on 16 September that the administrative work on which process would be used was still pending and would be announced formally once confirmed <sup>[N68](#n68), [N69](#n69)</sup>.
- **15 October 2026:** Bangladesh announced that recruitment had restarted through agencies on Malaysia's list <sup>[7](#o7)</sup>.
- **31 October 2026:** medical checks were due to start but did not, because no list of approved centres had been published <sup>[12](#o12), [N70](#n70)</sup>.
- **4 November 2026:** the minister said a list of 46 centres had gone to Malaysia for vetting <sup>[N18](#n18)</sup>.
- **From 8 November 2026:** see Status <sup>[N5](#n5), [N4](#n4), [N3](#n3)</sup>.

> **Need to know**
> - What, if anything, opened on 6 July 2026, the date the minister gave for the reopening <sup>[N31](#n31)</sup>?

<a id="the-channels"></a>

#### 1.2.3 The channels

- **A private agency on Malaysia's list:** closed since 31 May 2024; a restart through listed agencies was announced on 15 October 2026 and medical tests had started by 19 November (see [Status](#status)) <sup>[N2 (2024)](#n2), [7](#o7), [N3](#n3)</sup>. It is the route nearly everyone used in 2022-24 (numbers: see [Size](#size); cost: see [What people really pay](#what-people-really-pay); how the job passes through the agencies: see [How a job reaches you](#how-a-job-reaches-you-and-who-takes-a-cut), and who takes a cut) <sup>[N11](#n11), [N71 (2024)](#n71)</sup>. Until the restart, nothing was open through them <sup>[12](#o12), [N18](#n18)</sup>. The minister told parliament on 23 June 2026 that the government itself sends no workers to Malaysia and that workers can go through government-approved recruiting agencies <sup>[N63](#n63)</sup>.

  > **Need to know**
  > - Did the minister's statement of 23 June 2026 that the government itself sends no workers to Malaysia <sup>[N63](#n63)</sup> mean BOESL had stopped sending, while stranded 2024 workers were still due to go through it <sup>[N72](#n72)</sup>?

- **Through a relative in Malaysia:** a relative can find the job, but the visa still goes through a listed agency, so this route is open only as far as the agency route is ([Step 1, item 5: Or through a relative or friend in Malaysia](#step-1-find-a-real-job-item-5)) <sup>[7](#o7)</sup>. For example, Abu Taher, who had worked 10 years at the same company in Malaysia, took his nephew Abdus Salam there about a year and a half before October 2024 because the company was good, relatives said <sup>[N73 (2024)](#n73)</sup>.
- **BOESL:** circulars on boesl.gov.bd, a lottery among OEP-registered applicants, then the employer's interview; zero-cost by BOESL's and the minister's account (disputed: see [Status](#status)), but few places: it sent only 1,381 under the 2022-24 round ([Step 1, item 3: Apply for government (BOESL) jobs](#step-1-find-a-real-job-item-3)) <sup>[8](#o8), [N6](#n6), [N71 (2024)](#n71)</sup>. BOESL said on 16 May 2024 that it had been given the chance to send 10,000 workers at zero migration cost, had received demand for 1,695, and had sent 1,308 since October 2022 (331 in 2022, 839 in 2023 and 138 in 2024) <sup>[N74 (2024)](#n74)</sup>. It had demand for more than 10,000 workers, but had taken only 1,467 clearances by 24 April 2024 <sup>[N75 (2024)](#n75)</sup>, and Prothom Alo put those it sent at about one and a half thousand <sup>[N76](#n76)</sup>. Two BOESL officials told Prothom Alo that because its workers went free, at the employer's cost, obstacles came in many ways: demand letters went into other agencies' names and took time to bring back, and BOESL itself had to spend money to get demand letters for employer-paid recruitment <sup>[N75 (2024)](#n75), [N76](#n76)</sup>. The 73 BOESL workers flying on 17 May 2024 at the employer's cost were to earn RM1,500 (then Tk 37,500) a month with housing from the company; BOESL said it holds job fairs around the country, selects marginal and farm workers, and trains them on the work, the Malaysian language and culture, and sending money home legally <sup>[N74 (2024)](#n74)</sup>. Its then managing director, Mallik Anwar Hossain, said in May 2024 that under the agreement, if a worker works properly for three years, another member of his family can go to the same company at no cost <sup>[N74 (2024)](#n74)</sup>. For 2026 BOESL has the DHL call and demand for 558 more employer-paid workers (see [Status](#status)) <sup>[8](#o8), [N5](#n5)</sup>.

  > **Need to know**
  > - How many workers did BOESL send to Malaysia in the 2022-24 round: 1,381 <sup>[N71 (2024)](#n71)</sup>, about 1,500 <sup>[N76](#n76)</sup>, the 1,467 cleared by 24 April 2024 <sup>[N75 (2024)](#n75)</sup>, or the 1,308 BOESL gave on 16 May 2024 <sup>[N74 (2024)](#n74)</sup>?
  > - Did BOESL have demand for more than 10,000 workers in 2024 <sup>[N75 (2024)](#n75)</sup> or for 1,695 <sup>[N74 (2024)](#n74)</sup>?
  > - Is the 2024 promise that a family member can follow a worker who completes three years still honoured, and how does the family apply <sup>[N74 (2024)](#n74)</sup>?

- **CLAB (Construction Labour Exchange Centre Berhad, Malaysia's government construction recruiter), through BOESL:** construction only; it rejected about a third of those it interviewed on health and suitability <sup>[N25](#n25)</sup>.
- **Employment Pass:** professionals on at least RM5,000 a month <sup>[18](#o18)</sup>.
- **No legal route:** domestic work (Bangladesh is not an approved source country) <sup>[9](#o9), [19](#o19)</sup>; women in general jobs (no agreement exists, and the High Commission in Kuala Lumpur tells women not to go) <sup>[N20](#n20)</sup>; and jobs in Sabah, where there is no recruitment agreement and rings collect passports and cash <sup>[N77](#n77)</sup>. In a Facebook warning of 1 August 2025, the High Commission in Kuala Lumpur said an organised ring was collecting passports and cash by promising Sabah jobs, that the two governments have no agreement or understanding on recruiting for Sabah and the Sabah authorities have not decided to hire Bangladeshi workers, and that any permission would be announced on the High Commission's website <sup>[N78](#n78)</sup>. The main irregular route is a tourist visa used to work (see [What goes wrong most](#what-goes-wrong-most-and-what-people-wrongly-believe), and what people wrongly believe) <sup>[N79](#n79)</sup>.

<a id="skill-tests-and-narrower-job-orders"></a>

#### 1.2.4 Skill tests and narrower job orders

There is no skill test except in construction, where since January 2025 a worker needs a CIDB (Construction Industry Development Board) skills certificate, tested at Khulna Technical Training Centre ([Step 1, item 2: Learn a trade if you need one](#step-1-find-a-real-job-item-2)) <sup>[5](#o5), [20 (2024)](#o20), [21](#o21)</sup>. Job orders can be narrower, such as men aged 22 to 35 with SSC or HSC in BOESL's DHL circular ([Step 1, item 3: Apply for government (BOESL) jobs](#step-1-find-a-real-job-item-3)) <sup>[8](#o8)</sup>.

Bangladesh is pressing Malaysia to take more skilled workers. On 15 May 2025 it proposed security guards, nurses and caregivers, Asif Nazrul said <sup>[N40](#n40)</sup>. Before the June 2026 visit, Bangladesh's High Commissioner to Malaysia, Manjurul Karim Khan Chowdhury, said Malaysia's new industrial policy is changing its hiring, that the market for the kind of workers Bangladesh sends is shrinking, and that Bangladesh had started contacts on recruiting skilled and semi-skilled workers <sup>[N80](#n80)</sup>. At the 22 June 2026 talks Tarique Rahman asked Malaysia to recruit skilled workers from Bangladesh, and in August 2026 the two tourism ministries discussed qualified workers for hotels and tourism (see [How the restart came about](#how-the-restart-came-about)) <sup>[N56](#n56), [N66](#n66)</sup>.

<a id="size"></a>

#### 1.2.5 Size

Bangladesh is Malaysia's largest source of foreign workers, and Malaysia has for a decade been regarded as Bangladesh's second destination after Saudi Arabia, though it was not in the top five in 2025 <sup>[22](#o22), [N81](#n81), [N80](#n80), [N82](#n82)</sup>.

**Bangladeshis working in Malaysia:**

- About 8 lakh Bangladeshis held Malaysian work passes at the end of 2025, more than any other nationality <sup>[22](#o22)</sup>. Malaysia's Home Ministry told its parliament that 803,332 Bangladeshis held valid work permits at the end of June 2025, 37% of all foreign workers, making Bangladesh the largest source of unskilled foreign workers, The Star reported <sup>[N81](#n81)</sup>. Prothom Alo put about eight lakh Bangladeshis working in Malaysia in October 2024, and the foreign workforce at more than 21 lakh in September 2025, over 38% of it Bangladeshi <sup>[N83 (2024)](#n83), [N84](#n84)</sup>.
- In January 2024 more than 70% of foreign workers came from Bangladesh, Indonesia and Nepal, and the rest from India, Vietnam, Pakistan and Thailand, Prothom Alo reported <sup>[N85 (2024)](#n85)</sup>.
- Counts of all Bangladeshis in Malaysia, documented or not, run from 10 to 15 lakh: about 14 lakh plus about 5,000 students (a July 2024 press release) <sup>[N86 (2024)](#n86)</sup>; about 15 lakh (a Bangladeshi with 21 years in Malaysia, November 2024) <sup>[N87 (2024)](#n87)</sup>; about 12 lakh (a column of October 2025) <sup>[N88](#n88)</sup>; about 10 lakh workers, legal and illegal (Bangladeshis doing specialised work there, June 2026) <sup>[N80](#n80)</sup>. Bangladeshis in Malaysia told Prothom Alo in December 2025 that about twice as many work there as hold permits, many having gone on student visas and stayed, and an IOM (International Organization for Migration) report said 25 lakh people who entered Malaysia to travel had not left by 2024, 38% of them Bangladeshi <sup>[N45](#n45)</sup>.
- Bangladeshis are seen at every tourist spot in Cameron Highlands, Pahang, and dominate its vegetable farms, a February 2025 piece in Prothom Alo said <sup>[N89](#n89)</sup>.

> **Need to know**
> - How many Bangladeshis are in Malaysia without a valid pass, and how many of them first entered on student or visit visas?

**Departures from Bangladesh:**

- By BMET data cited by Prothom Alo, 12.5 lakh went legally from 1992 to January 2024, more than 3.5 lakh of them in 2023 and 50,000 in 2022 <sup>[N38 (2024)](#n38)</sup>. By the figures Prothom Alo gave in December 2025: 50,090 in 2022, a record 3,51,683 in 2023, 93,632 in 2024 before the ban, and 2,486 in 2025 up to May <sup>[N45](#n45)</sup>. Malaysia's Home Ministry counted 49,353 Bangladeshi workers entering in 2022 and 397,548 in 2023 <sup>[N81](#n81)</sup>.
- 2024: fewer than one lakh went in the whole year, a fall of more than two lakh on 2023, after the market closed in June (BMET data, Prothom Alo) <sup>[N90](#n90)</sup>; 93,000 by the annual report of the digital migration platform Ami Probashi, which named Malaysia's new labour policy as the main reason <sup>[N91](#n91)</sup>.
- The 2022-24 round: about 4.5 lakh went between August 2022 and May 2024 <sup>[N11](#n11), [N83 (2024)](#n83)</sup>. The Ministry's committee report says Malaysia allocated Bangladesh a quota of 5,32,162, the Ministry gave recruitment permission for 5,27,999, agencies took BMET smart cards for 4,93,729, and 17,777 with clearance (3.66%) could not go <sup>[N92](#n92)</sup>. At a Ministry press conference on 2 June 2024, 101 agencies were said to have got approval for 5,26,676 workers, BMET cleared 4,93,642, 4,76,672 went and 16,970 could not go in time <sup>[N93 (2024)](#n93)</sup>; on 4 July 2024 the Ministry gave 5 lakh 32 thousand approved, 4 lakh 76 thousand gone and 17,000 with clearance who could not go <sup>[N94 (2024)](#n94)</sup>.

> **Need to know**
> - How many Bangladeshis went to Malaysia in 2023: the 3,51,683 by Bangladesh's count <sup>[N45](#n45)</sup> or the 397,548 entries Malaysia counted <sup>[N81](#n81)</sup>?
> - How many went in the 2022-24 round: about 4.5 lakh <sup>[N11](#n11)</sup> or 4,76,672 <sup>[N93 (2024)](#n93)</sup>?

**Against other destinations:**

- 2019-23: 97% of Bangladesh's workers went to 10 countries, and Malaysia was one of eight that took more than 10,000 a year on average (BMET data) <sup>[N95 (2024)](#n95)</sup>.
- 2023: Malaysia was the second-largest destination after Saudi Arabia, in a year when more than 13 lakh workers left Bangladesh, the most ever, RMMRU (Refugee and Migratory Movements Research Unit, a Dhaka research body) reported <sup>[N96 (2024)](#n96)</sup>.
- 2024: 90% of the 10 lakh who went abroad went to Saudi Arabia, Malaysia, Qatar, Singapore and the UAE; with Malaysia and the UAE closed and Oman stopped, Saudi Arabia was the only large market open in January 2025 <sup>[N90](#n90)</sup>. Malaysia stayed second, Ami Probashi said <sup>[N91](#n91)</sup>. President Mohammed Shahabuddin called it the second-largest destination when meeting Anwar Ibrahim on 4 October 2024 and asked Malaysia to take more <sup>[N97 (2024)](#n97)</sup>.
- 2025: of 11,30,757 who went abroad, 67% went to Saudi Arabia, then Qatar (10%), Singapore (6%), Kuwait (4%) and the Maldives (4%), RMMRU reported <sup>[N82](#n82)</sup>.
- 2026: BOESL's managing director still called Malaysia Bangladesh's second-largest labour market on 12 November <sup>[N98](#n98)</sup>. BAIRA's general members estimated in April 2025 that a reopened Malaysia could take at least 2 lakh Bangladeshis a year, 10 lakh in five years, bringing at least USD 500 crore a year in remittances <sup>[N37](#n37)</sup>.

**In 2026, before the restart:** BMET cleared 1,592 workers for Malaysia from 1 January to 6 November 2026, though the restart had sent no one by then <sup>[13](#o13), [N18](#n18), [12](#o12)</sup>. CLAB accepted about 800 construction workers in December 2025 and January 2026 <sup>[N25](#n25)</sup>; 120 to 150 stranded 2024 workers were still waiting to go through BOESL in October 2026 (see [What closed it before](#what-closed-it-before)) <sup>[N72](#n72)</sup>.

> **Need to know**
> - Who were the 1,592 workers BMET cleared for Malaysia in 2026: stranded 2024 workers, CLAB construction workers, or others?

<a id="what-closed-it-before"></a>

#### 1.2.6 What closed it before

Malaysia has closed to Bangladesh three times in 15 years, Prothom Alo reported on 31 May 2024 <sup>[N75 (2024)](#n75)</sup>. Each closure came after a surge: more than 4 lakh went in 2007-08 before the first, about 3 lakh in 2017-18 before the second, and about 5 lakh from August 2022 to May 2024 before the third; about 13 lakh went from 2004 to May 2024 <sup>[N17](#n17)</sup>. The first 23 Bangladeshi workers went in 1978, and the formal manpower agreement was signed in 1992 <sup>[N45](#n45)</sup>. Three North South University researchers wrote in October 2024 that Bangladesh had tried business-to-business (B2B), government-to-government (G2G) and G-plus-B2B models and none had yet worked, and that powerful syndicates, brokers and middlemen forced high costs on workers <sup>[N99 (2024)](#n99)</sup>.

- **2009:** Malaysia froze hiring after about 4 lakh Bangladeshis went in 2007-08 and many found no job and became undocumented; the closure followed reports of labour exploitation and allegations that agencies had formed syndicates <sup>[N100](#n100), [N15 (2018)](#n15), [N2 (2024)](#n2)</sup>. Agents had charged over Tk 2 lakh against a fixed Tk 84,000 <sup>[N15 (2018)](#n15)</sup>. Selim Reza dates this closure to 2008 <sup>[N101](#n101)</sup>.
- **September 2018:** Malaysia's new government cancelled the 10-agency "G2G Plus" scheme from 1 September, calling it monopolistic, amid allegations of syndication and corruption <sup>[N15 (2018)](#n15), [N2 (2024)](#n2)</sup>. About two lakh workers had gone through those 10 agencies, which were alleged to take Tk 3 to 4 lakh from each against a government-fixed Tk 37,000 <sup>[N15 (2018)](#n15)</sup>. The round had opened at the end of 2016 <sup>[N2 (2024)](#n2), [N75 (2024)](#n75)</sup>. Prothom Alo reported in 2024 that its online system went in 2016 to a firm named Synerflux, whose owner it names as Dato Seri Abdul bin Amin Nur, a Malaysian citizen of Bangladeshi origin; that with his help the 10 agencies formed a ring; that workers spent Tk 2 to 3.5 lakh against a set cost of Tk 33,375; and that the ring took about Tk 5,500 crore in two years <sup>[N38 (2024)](#n38)</sup>.
- **31 May 2024:** the last round, under the 2021 MoU, ended (details below) <sup>[16 (2021)](#o16), [N2 (2024)](#n2)</sup>.

> **Need to know**
> - Did Malaysia stop recruiting from Bangladesh in 2008 <sup>[N101](#n101)</sup> or 2009 <sup>[N100](#n100)</sup>?
> - Was the government-fixed cost in the 2016-18 round Tk 37,000 <sup>[N15 (2018)](#n15)</sup> or Tk 33,375 <sup>[N38 (2024)](#n38)</sup>?
> - Did about two lakh <sup>[N15 (2018)](#n15)</sup> or about three lakh <sup>[N17](#n17)</sup> go in the 2016-18 round?

**Why Malaysia closed in 2024.** Malaysia set 31 May 2024 as the last day for new workers to enter after tens of thousands had paid up to USD 5,000 for jobs that did not exist <sup>[16 (2021)](#o16), [N2 (2024)](#n2), [N102 (2024)](#n102)</sup>. On 16 January 2024 its human resources minister, Steven Sim, and home minister, Saifuddin Ismail, said Malaysia was reviewing its foreign-worker agreements with 15 countries including Bangladesh because labour was unevenly spread across the economy, and the home minister said recruitment fees, other costs, contract clauses and health would be reviewed <sup>[N85 (2024)](#n85)</sup>. Malaysia stopped approving new applications to hire foreign workers; visa applications for workers already approved had to be filed by 31 March 2024 and those with visas had to enter by 31 May, Prothom Alo reported on 21 March 2024, citing Ministry sources <sup>[N38 (2024)](#n38)</sup>. From 1 June 2024 no worker from any other source country could enter either, and Prothom Alo reported that the other countries blamed the Bangladeshi syndicate <sup>[N38 (2024)](#n38)</sup>. The explanations given:

- a Prothom Alo editorial of 24 March 2024: pressure on Malaysia's government from international human rights organisations <sup>[N103 (2024)](#n103)</sup>;
- three North South University researchers, 10 June 2024: to curb recruitment irregularities and frame policy, mainly as part of re-evaluating labour policy and filling the foreign-worker quota set in the 12th Malaysia Plan <sup>[N104 (2024)](#n104)</sup>;
- Prothom Alo editorial of 6 January 2025 and report of May 2025: allegations of a syndicate in sending workers, which closed the market for all source countries <sup>[N105](#n105), [N17](#n17)</sup>.

**The last weeks, May 2024:**

- The Dhaka-Kuala Lumpur fare, normally Tk 20,000 to 25,000 at most, rose to Tk 80,000 to 1 lakh by mid-May, above Tk 1 lakh by Prothom Alo's later report, and all seats from 27 to 31 May showed sold out on Biman's website <sup>[N106 (2024)](#n106), [N107](#n107)</sup>. Ethar Fariel Hamid, managing director of Fariel Tours and Travels, complained to the Prime Minister's Office on 26 May that Biman's special-flight tickets were not shown on the GDS (global distribution system, the booking network travel agents use) and opened only for a bribe of Tk 20,000 to 25,000 a ticket, pushing the price to Tk 1.05 to 1.10 lakh; Biman sources said special-flight fares started at Tk 68,000 and its acting marketing director said 99% were sold through the GDS <sup>[N106 (2024)](#n106)</sup>.
- The civil aviation minister, Muhammad Faruk Khan, said on 30 May that Biman was running three or four flights a day, that agencies had known a month ahead that 31 May was the last date but made no arrangements, and that a charter on an Air Cambodia aircraft had been allowed on 29 May <sup>[N108 (2024)](#n108)</sup>. The state minister for expatriates' welfare, Shafiqur Rahman Chowdhury, said on 1 June that BAIRA could not give the Ministry a list of who had visas and when they would fly, which caused the flight problems, and that the Ministry had arranged about 22 special flights <sup>[N109 (2024)](#n109)</sup>.
- Malaysia issued visas to the end, some on 30 May, Ruhul Amin, alias Swapan, a former BAIRA secretary general whom the article calls a businessman at the centre of the Malaysia syndicate, said at a 4 June briefing held on behalf of the panel of 101 agencies; BAIRA's secretary general, Ali Haider Chowdhury, said the rush would not have happened had Malaysia stopped visas from 15 May <sup>[N110 (2024)](#n110)</sup>. Malaysia's High Commissioner in Dhaka, Haznah Md Hashim, said on 5 June that the deadline would not be extended because it applied to all 15 source countries, and that Malaysia could not accept without proof BAIRA's allegation that e-visas had been issued on 2 June <sup>[N111 (2024)](#n111)</sup>.
- Near the end Malaysia blacklisted 47 companies over complaints, so workers with visas under them could not go, the panel of 101 agencies said on 4 June <sup>[N110 (2024)](#n110)</sup>. The expatriates' welfare secretary said the Ministry had not allowed workers to be sent in the names of blacklisted companies <sup>[N93 (2024)](#n93)</sup>.
- On 31 May thousands crowded Dhaka airport, many without tickets <sup>[N112 (2024)](#n112)</sup>. Habibur Rahman of Natore, who had paid Tk 6.5 lakh raised by mortgaging land and a bank loan, had waited three days at the airport while the firm stopped answering his calls; Rubel Mia of Narsingdi had paid Tk 6 lakh to a firm named Ahad Enterprise to go as a construction worker <sup>[N112 (2024)](#n112)</sup>. Tanvir Mia (22) of Debgram, Akhaura, Brahmanbaria, for whom Tk 6.5 lakh had been paid a year earlier to broker Alamgir Hossain of Nayadil village and his brother in Malaysia for a supermarket job, according to his uncle, had a January flight and another at the end of May cancelled; on 31 May he fell from the train home into the Meghna at Bhairab and went missing, and rescuers believed he had drowned <sup>[N113 (2024)](#n113)</sup>.

**What happened to the stranded workers of 2024:**

- About 17,777 workers who had BMET clearance could not fly by the deadline <sup>[N16 (2024)](#n16)</sup>. A six-member Ministry committee formed on 2 June 2024 under Additional Secretary (Employment) Nur Md Mahbubul Haque found that the failure lay entirely with the recruiting agencies concerned, and that some agencies took Ministry permission but never applied for BMET smart cards for 34,270 workers, which it called clear negligence <sup>[N93 (2024)](#n93), [N92](#n92)</sup>.
- Other counts differ. By a Prothom Alo column of 4 June 2024, the Ministry and BMET put those with final clearance at 16,970, a BMET source said 31,304 in all could not go because of visa and approval complications, and BAIRA officials said about 40,000 <sup>[N114 (2024)](#n114)</sup>. At the 4 June 2024 briefing for the 101 agencies, it was said that 2 to 3% of cleared workers usually fail to go; Ruhul Amin Swapan, owner of Catharsis International (see [Who controls it](#who-controls-it)), put the real number at 5,000 to 6,000 and said that if diplomatic efforts to send them failed their costs would be returned and those affected could apply to the original agency for their money; BAIRA's secretary general said the number was not certain and BAIRA would help the Ministry's committee list them <sup>[N110 (2024)](#n110)</sup>. BAIRA members said in October 2024 and April 2025 that about 50,000 who had paid and were in process were harmed, 17,000 of whom had completed every step <sup>[N115 (2024)](#n115), [N29](#n29)</sup>. Anwar Ibrahim, the protesting workers and Malaysia's The Edge spoke of about 18,000 <sup>[N83 (2024)](#n83), [N116](#n116), [N54](#n54)</sup>; Prothom Alo reported 15,000 in August 2025 <sup>[N44](#n44)</sup>. A Prothom Alo column of 2 June 2025 said Malaysia had put at about 11,000 those unable to go despite valid calling visas, while agency owners and the press put the number much higher <sup>[N117](#n117)</sup>.
- Of the 16,970 with final clearance, some could not get air tickets and others did not get the employer's final consent: the employer had not sent its assurance that it would receive them at the airport, Prothom Alo reported <sup>[N118](#n118)</sup>.

> **Need to know**
> - How many workers were stranded by the 31 May 2024 deadline: 16,970 <sup>[N114 (2024)](#n114)</sup>, 17,777 <sup>[N16 (2024)](#n16)</sup>, about 18,000 <sup>[N83 (2024)](#n83)</sup>, 31,304 <sup>[N114 (2024)](#n114)</sup>, about 40,000 <sup>[N114 (2024)](#n114)</sup>, about 50,000 <sup>[N115 (2024)](#n115)</sup>, 5,000 to 6,000 <sup>[N110 (2024)](#n110)</sup>, 15,000 <sup>[N44](#n44)</sup>, or about 11,000 with valid calling visas <sup>[N117](#n117)</sup>?

**Complaints and refunds, 2024-25:**

- **2 June 2024:** anyone could complain to the Ministry's committee, including workers charged more than the Tk 78,990 cap; it was to report within 7 working days and work to get affected workers compensated <sup>[N93 (2024)](#n93)</sup>. The expatriates' welfare secretary, Md Ruhul Amin, said agencies would refund those who got clearance but could not go, that BAIRA had taken responsibility for this, and that agencies that did not would face legal action <sup>[N93 (2024)](#n93)</sup>. The state minister said on 5 June that those holding a BMET card or e-visa who could not go would get compensation, and on 9 June, after complaints closed, that the committee's report would recommend penalties <sup>[N111 (2024)](#n111), [N119 (2024)](#n119)</sup>.
- **June to July 2024:** about 3,000 complaints reached the Ministry, some covering several workers, the state minister told parliament; the Ministry later said 2,055 workers had complained with their papers <sup>[N120 (2024)](#n120), [N121 (2024)](#n121), [N94 (2024)](#n94)</sup>. On 2 July the Ministry told parliament's standing committee that the report submitted on 24 June was incomplete, and the committee, chaired by Imran Ahmad, asked it to establish exactly how many could not go <sup>[N122 (2024)](#n122)</sup>.
- **4 July 2024:** BAIRA agreed that agencies would refund by 18 July as much as each worker could prove he had paid; the state minister said more or less all of the 100 approved agencies were responsible and that those workers would go first when the market reopened, and Prothom Alo reported that more or less all 100 agencies held the stranded workers' papers <sup>[N94 (2024)](#n94)</sup>.
- **25 July 2024:** BAIRA told the state minister that 70% had got their money back, but the number could not yet be given: each worker was to confirm in writing once paid and BAIRA was to submit the list <sup>[N123 (2024)](#n123)</sup>. A letter had gone to Malaysia about the stranded workers <sup>[N123 (2024)](#n123)</sup>.
- **24 September 2024:** Asif Nazrul said it had been confirmed that 25% of the 17,000 had got their money back, and that refunds to all would be ensured <sup>[N124 (2024)](#n124)</sup>.
- **Two days before 22 January 2025:** agencies had refunded 81% of the stranded workers and submitted lists with the workers' signatures, with a deadline of 23 January for the rest, the expatriates' welfare secretary said <sup>[N116](#n116)</sup>.
- **27 April 2025:** the committee's report, which recommended legal action against the agencies, immediate refunds, and settlement of complaints of charging more than Tk 78,990, was filed in the High Court in a writ by Supreme Court lawyer Tanvir Ahmed; a bench of Justices Fahmida Quader and Mubina Asaf ordered a progress report on action taken by 27 August 2025 <sup>[N92](#n92)</sup>. The government side said the stranded workers would be taken on a priority basis, and the court asked what had been done to refund in full those charged more than Tk 78,990, the petitioner's lawyer said <sup>[N92](#n92)</sup>.
- Many got no refund and some a small one <sup>[N125](#n125)</sup>.

**Getting them to Malaysia, 2024-25:**

- **4 October 2024:** at a joint press conference in Dhaka with the Chief Adviser, Anwar Ibrahim said his government would give immediate attention to 18,000 Bangladeshi workers going in a first phase, subject to all conditions being met, and that workers must not be treated as modern slaves <sup>[N83 (2024)](#n83), [N126 (2024)](#n126)</sup>. Asif Nazrul said the next day that these were the stranded workers, not new ones, and that a promise does not mean certainty <sup>[N34 (2024)](#n34)</sup>.
- **5 December 2024 to January 2025:** the High Commissioner met Malaysia's home minister, Saifuddin Nasution Ismail, who proposed a Joint Technical Group of Malaysian Immigration and High Commission representatives to take the workers in phases; it met on 31 December 2024 and 14 January 2025, and after checking its list of more than 17,000 Bangladesh named 7,964 as eligible for the first phase, the foreign ministry said <sup>[N127](#n127), [N128](#n128)</sup>. Yunus asked Malaysia in January 2025 to let the 18,000 in <sup>[N128](#n128)</sup>.
- **22 January 2025:** about a hundred stranded workers, some wearing burial shrouds, blocked one side of the SAARC Fountain intersection at Karwan Bazar, Dhaka, from 9 am, chanting against the syndicate and demanding to go to Malaysia, then moved to the Ministry after police cleared them <sup>[N116](#n116)</sup>. The secretary said Malaysia had agreed in principle, that the list had been sent, that the method was not yet fixed, that sending would start within 15 days of Malaysia's decision and the workers would go in phases, and that efforts were being made so they would not face new costs <sup>[N116](#n116)</sup>.
- **15 to 21 May 2025:** Asif Nazrul said Malaysia had confirmed a first batch of 7,926 <sup>[N40](#n40)</sup>; Lutfey Siddiqi said about 8,000 would go first, through BOESL <sup>[N41](#n41)</sup>.
- **20 August 2025:** the Ministry's first-year report and the adviser said an agreement had been signed to send 8,000 in an initial phase, which BOESL had taken on at nominal cost <sup>[N44](#n44), [N129](#n129)</sup>.
- A revised High Commission notice of 1 August 2025 said Malaysia had approved employment through BOESL for workers among those who could not come by 31 May 2024, in construction or tourism <sup>[N118](#n118)</sup>. Employers were to submit the demand-letter attestation checklist for tourism workers on FWCMS, apply for construction workers through CLAB onto the same portal, and lodge the originals and one set of photocopies with the High Commission <sup>[N118](#n118)</sup>. A Prothom Alo column of 2 June 2025 said the expatriates' welfare adviser had recently said Malaysia would at first arrange work for about eight thousand of them, though no visible step had yet been taken <sup>[N117](#n117)</sup>.
- Malaysia cleared 7,873 of them to go through BOESL, which charged them another Tk 1,62,500 each <sup>[N125](#n125), [17](#o17), [N130](#n130)</sup>.
- The first of them flew on 25 November 2025, 18 months after the deadline <sup>[17](#o17), [N131](#n131)</sup>.

> **Need to know**
> - Was the first phase 7,873 <sup>[17](#o17)</sup>, 7,964 <sup>[N127](#n127)</sup>, 7,926 <sup>[N40](#n40)</sup> or about 8,000 <sup>[N41](#n41)</sup> workers?
> - How many of them had flown by November 2026?
> - Were CLAB's 800 construction workers of December 2025 to January 2026 among them?

**The plantation window, November 2024 to January 2025.** A Ministry notice reported on 4 November 2024 announced recruitment for a plantation sector in Malaysia, with 31 January 2025 as the last date to enter <sup>[N132 (2024)](#n132)</sup>. The medical, visa, BMET clearance, pre-departure orientation and air ticket all had to be done by 15 January 2025, and no money beyond the government-fixed Tk 78,990 was to be paid to anyone <sup>[N132 (2024)](#n132)</sup>. Workers were told to learn the job terms, pay and benefits from the recruiting agent, to deal money with no one but an approved agent, to pay only against a written receipt or through a bank account, and to verify the agent on the Ministry's website or through the call centre 16135 <sup>[N132 (2024)](#n132)</sup>.

<a id="how-a-job-reaches-you-and-who-takes-a-cut"></a>

### 1.3 How a job reaches you, and who takes a cut

A job reaches a village down a chain, and every link takes a cut: the employer's quota becomes calling visas; the employer's demand letter is bought in Malaysia; the visa is processed in FWCMS; then a licensed agency; then a village broker (দালাল) <sup>[9](#o9), [N8](#n8), [N7](#n7), [7](#o7), [N9 (2024)](#n9), [N10 (2018)](#n10)</sup>. The choke point is FWCMS, built by the company Bestinet: every Bangladeshi worker's visa passes through it, and only BOESL and 25 private agencies chosen by Malaysia can process a visa in it, so every other agency pays one of them <sup>[23](#o23), [N133](#n133), [7](#o7), [N8](#n8)</sup>. On the street the whole arrangement is called the সিন্ডিকেট (syndicate) <sup>[N134](#n134)</sup>.

<a id="the-quota"></a>

#### 1.3.1 The quota

Quotas are approved on the employer's paper eligibility, not on a checked need, so a quota, and the visa sold from it, can exist with no job behind it; Malaysia's private employment agencies' association says agents apply for 100 workers when a company needs 10 <sup>[N135 (2023)](#n135), [N136 (2023)](#n136)</sup>. The employer pays a yearly levy per worker, RM1,850 in manufacturing, construction and services and RM640 in plantation and agriculture in Peninsular Malaysia <sup>[9](#o9), [24](#o24)</sup>. In October 2026 Immigration broke up an "A-to-B" ring, believed to be run by two Bangladeshi men, that got quotas on false company details and hired the workers out to other firms at RM3,800 to 5,000 profit a head <sup>[N137](#n137), [N138](#n138)</sup>. In that raid, on 8 October 2026, Immigration's Director General, Datuk Zakaria Shaban, said the team seized 265 Bangladeshi passports (with 63 Indonesian, 12 Pakistani and 1 Indian) and RM6,700 believed paid by customers, detained 9 Bangladeshi men among 12 foreign "customers", and found that the workers brought in did not work for the companies that applied for the quota but independently for other companies <sup>[N139](#n139)</sup>.

<a id="which-employer"></a>

#### 1.3.2 Which employer

The employer decides whether a job and pay are waiting when you land, and whether you get your recruitment fee back. The ones to avoid are firms whose quota is far above the work they have: they have left workers with no job. The ones that have paid fees back are large export manufacturers under pressure from foreign buyers <sup>[N140 (2023)](#n140), [N141 (2021)](#n141), [N142 (2024)](#n142)</sup>.

- **Bad: ghost jobs:** services firms brought in 142,204 workers in 2023 against 20,000 needed; 161 Bangladeshis promised construction jobs had no work or pay for seven months; and a Kuala Lumpur company hired about 600 and gave them no work <sup>[N140 (2023)](#n140), [N143 (2024)](#n143), [N144 (2024)](#n144)</sup>. In January 2024 Malaysia's human resources minister said the authorities had finished investigating five companies accused of promising hundreds of workers jobs and not giving them, that employers who hired such workers must pay their wages whether or not there is work, and that companies and people who break the law will not be allowed to hire migrant workers <sup>[N85 (2024)](#n85)</sup>. In a statement of 20 March 2024, BCSM (Bangladesh Civil Society for Migrants, a coalition of 23 organisations including RMMRU and BRAC) said some syndicate agencies had sent workers to Malaysian firms with no capacity to employ them, and in some cases to companies that did not exist under that name, after taking lakhs of taka from each worker <sup>[N145 (2024)](#n145), [N39](#n39)</sup>.
- **No work on arrival, 2024-25:**
  - **February 2024:** at least 63 young men from Kazipur and Motmura unions in Gangni upazila, Meherpur, who went in November 2023, said they had been held for three months in a three-storey building in Kuala Lumpur with their passports taken, no work, visas mostly expired, not allowed out and fed only potatoes, dal and rice twice a day <sup>[N146 (2024)](#n146)</sup>.
  - **March 2024:** two officials of the Bangladesh mission in Malaysia, speaking anonymously, told Prothom Alo that 1.5 to 2 lakh Bangladeshi workers there were unemployed and living in inhuman conditions, that many came to the mission regularly for help, and that the mission could do little <sup>[N38 (2024)](#n38)</sup>. That month a member of the Facebook group "Malaysia Probashi Bangladeshi" wrote that his newly arrived brother had found no work for four months <sup>[N38 (2024)](#n38)</sup>. From January to 24 March 2024, 453 workers came home empty-handed and without passports, on travel permits <sup>[N147 (2024)](#n147)</sup>.
  - **May 2024:** the Ministry's own count was five thousand who had found no work, while long-settled Bangladeshis said thousands were jobless, some selling goods on pavements or umbrellas in the rain to pay for food <sup>[N147 (2024)](#n147)</sup>.
  - **2 June 2024:** the Ministry's secretary said employers come to the Malaysian airport to collect workers, that in the previous few months none had failed to, and that work was being arranged for the five thousand who had found none <sup>[N93 (2024)](#n93)</sup>.
  - **June 2024:** Md Rajib of Shibchar, Madaripur, who went about three months earlier after spending Tk 4 lakh 90 thousand, had still found no job, was living with an uncle in Kuala Lumpur for food and lodging, and had sent no money home <sup>[N148 (2024)](#n148)</sup>.
  - **November 2025:** a group of workers from Cumilla, met near Sultan Abdul Samad Jamek Mosque in Kuala Lumpur, said each had spent Tk 5 lakh through a broker and they had still found no work, and were trying for factory jobs outside the capital <sup>[N45](#n45)</sup>.
- **Employers that avoid Bangladeshis:** three North South University researchers wrote in Prothom Alo on 10 June 2024 that some palm oil producers in Malaysia often refrain from recruiting Bangladeshi workers <sup>[N104 (2024)](#n104)</sup>.
- **Good: repaid fees:** after US import bans for forced labour, Top Glove paid workers over USD 30 million and FGV set aside RM112 million to reimburse fees to current and former migrant workers <sup>[N141 (2021)](#n141), [N142 (2024)](#n142)</sup>.
- **Employer-paid recruitment:** BCSM said in May 2025 that before the syndicate some Malaysian companies took workers from Bangladesh at the employer's cost but could no longer do so, and that some who went earlier found no job and lived half-starved abroad or on money sent from home <sup>[N39](#n39)</sup>.

<a id="company-visa-agent-visa-free-visa"></a>

#### 1.3.3 Company visa, agent visa, free visa

Workers sort jobs by who holds the visa; what separates the three is which company holds your quota and whether you work at its address <sup>[N149 (2024)](#n149), [N150](#n150)</sup>:

- **কোম্পানি ভিসা (company visa):** you work for the company that applied for you; it pays the visa and the yearly renewal, and keeps your passport <sup>[N149 (2024)](#n149)</sup>.
- **"Agent visa":** puts you under an agent or subcontractor who hires you out. Outsourcing companies were abolished in 2019, but some workers who go legally still land with one, are sent to work elsewhere, and can't tell until they arrive <sup>[N151 (2019)](#n151), [N152 (2023)](#n152)</sup>.
- **ফ্রি ভিসা (free visa):** sells the right to work anywhere, which no work pass gives. Every work pass (PLKS) is tied to the employer that applied, free-visa workers pay an agent about RM5,000 (Tk 1.53 lakh) a year to renew, and agents hold back weeks or months of their pay <sup>[5](#o5), [N149 (2024)](#n149)</sup>.

Working away from the company on your permit is an offence: Immigration's Ops Mahir raid in Ipoh in September 2026 detained 175 foreigners, mostly Bangladeshis, including people working outside the company named on their permit <sup>[5](#o5), [N150](#n150)</sup>. The calling visa (VDR, the visa approval) comes first and the e-visa after it, both through BOESL or one of the 25; check both ([Step 3: The employer gets your visa approval (VDR)](#step-3-the-employer-gets-your-visa-approval-vdr); [Step 5: Check the visa yourself](#step-5-check-the-visa-yourself)) <sup>[7](#o7), [5](#o5)</sup>.

<a id="the-demand-letter-the-first-price"></a>

#### 1.3.4 The demand letter: the first price

The demand letter is the employer's job order for a set number of workers, and buying it is the first cost in the chain: a Bangladeshi agency buys it from Malaysia <sup>[N28](#n28), [N7](#n7)</sup>. Agency owners say one now costs RM8,000 to 12,000 per worker (Tk 2.45 to 3.67 lakh), against RM2,000 to 3,000 before (2022-24 prices: see [Where a Tk 5.5 lakh deal goes](#where-a-tk-55-lakh-deal-goes)) <sup>[N7](#n7)</sup>. Big agencies buy the demand and pay the middlemen around it, so small agencies can't send workers cheaply <sup>[N7](#n7)</sup>. In 2022-24, clearances for 4,92,416 workers were taken in the names of the 100 syndicate agencies in a little over a year and a half, and at least half of those job letters are alleged to have been bought, at an average of RM6,000 (Tk 1.5 lakh) a worker, Prothom Alo reported in September 2024 <sup>[N153 (2024)](#n153)</sup>.

> **Need to know**
> - Who in Malaysia sells demand letters (the employer's own staff, Malaysian agents, Bangladeshi-born brokers)?
> - How is the money for a demand letter paid?
> - Did a 2022-24 demand letter cost about RM6,000 (Tk 1.5 lakh) a worker <sup>[N153 (2024)](#n153)</sup> or Tk 1.6 to 2 lakh <sup>[N11](#n11)</sup>?

<a id="attestation"></a>

#### 1.3.5 Attestation

Every demand letter must be attested by the Bangladesh High Commission in Kuala Lumpur, against the employer's notarised commitment to give the workers work and pay <sup>[N24](#n24), [25 (2023)](#o25)</sup>. Attestation is not proof that the job was checked: in 2019 the High Commissioner said the mission lacked the staff to check every case <sup>[N154 (2019)](#n154)</sup>.

> **Need to know**
> - What does the High Commission check before attesting a 2026 demand (a site visit, the quota approval, the levy receipt)?
> - How long does attestation take?

<a id="the-processing-agency-the-toll-gate"></a>

#### 1.3.6 The processing agency: the toll gate

Every visa must go through BOESL or one of 25 private "processing" agencies, which charge other agencies a fee per worker <sup>[7](#o7), [N155](#n155), [N8](#n8)</sup>. Malaysia lists 338 Bangladeshi agencies: BOESL, the 25 "processing" agencies, and 312 associates (সহযোগী এজেন্সি), 10 under each of the 25 and 62 under BOESL <sup>[7](#o7), [26](#o26), [N156](#n156)</sup>; Prothom Alo's summary of the Ministry's 15 October 2026 announcement put the number allowed to work at 337, including BOESL and the 25 <sup>[N21](#n21)</sup>. An associate may find employers, collect demand and select workers, but the calling visa and e-visa go through BOESL or one of the 25, which issues a no-objection certificate so the associate can get BMET clearance under its own licence <sup>[7](#o7)</sup>. For 2026, claims for the toll run from a Tk 6,000 processing fee to RM5,500 (about Tk 1.68 lakh), the figure BAIRA (Bangladesh Association of International Recruiting Agencies) members allege (2022-24 toll: see [Where a Tk 5.5 lakh deal goes](#where-a-tk-55-lakh-deal-goes)) <sup>[N155](#n155), [N8](#n8)</sup>. Even the government channel buys visas: of the Tk 1,62,500 BOESL charged stranded workers, its managing director said Tk 1 lakh was for buying visas <sup>[N130](#n130)</sup>.

> **Need to know**
> - Does Malaysia's list hold 338 Bangladeshi agencies <sup>[7](#o7)</sup> or 337 <sup>[N21](#n21)</sup>?

**How the toll worked in 2022-24.** Once a Malaysian demand letter was finalised, FWCMS distributed it among the approved agencies, not equally, and the workers had to be sent through the agency in whose name it arrived <sup>[N75 (2024)](#n75)</sup>. Agency owners outside the list told Prothom Alo that Malaysian companies sent demand letters to them, because of their long business there, but the letters went to a listed agency because their own names were not in FWCMS, so they paid that agency to send their workers: Tk 1.52 to 1.65 lakh a worker, three owners said in May 2024; Tk 1,52,000, four owners said in September 2026 <sup>[N147 (2024)](#n147), [N75 (2024)](#n75), [N76](#n76)</sup>. Prothom Alo reported that the agencies inside the syndicate took at least Tk 1.5 lakh in "syndicate fee" a worker without doing anything, an allegation the ACC (Anti-Corruption Commission) is investigating <sup>[N76](#n76)</sup>. BAIRA's joint secretary general, Fakhrul Islam, said in May 2024 that with no set rules for making the list, some agencies with no experience of sending workers got on it in collusion with the ringleaders and then earned Tk 1,52,000 a worker sitting at home <sup>[N75 (2024)](#n75)</sup>. In September 2024 he, also managing director of the agency Human Resource Development Centre, said the agency he sent workers through took Tk 1,52,000 a worker just for arranging the BMET clearance, which in fact costs Tk 6,000, and that the airfare and all the worker's other costs had to be paid separately <sup>[N153 (2024)](#n153)</sup>. BAIRA members' memoranda to the Ministry put the ring's take two ways: Tk 1,07,000 a person, extorted by the syndicate built by Ruhul Amin and Aminul Islam (5 February 2025) <sup>[N29](#n29)</sup>; and Tk 1,52,000 a worker, of which RM5,000 (Tk 1,25,000) went to Bestinet, collected in Bangladesh by Amin's local representative Ruhul Amin, with the worker's whole cost at Tk 4 to 5 lakh (21 April 2025, signed by the owners of 453 agencies) <sup>[N157](#n157)</sup>. Who these two men are: see [Who controls it](#who-controls-it).

> **Need to know**
> - Was the 2022-24 syndicate fee a flat Tk 1,52,000 a worker, or up to Tk 1,65,000 as three agency owners said in May 2024 <sup>[N147 (2024)](#n147)</sup>?
> - Of the fee, how much went on to Bestinet: Tk 1 lakh <sup>[N153 (2024)](#n153)</sup> or RM5,000 (Tk 1,25,000) <sup>[N157](#n157)</sup>?

Listed agencies said they only processed visas for others:

- **Sarkar International**, which sent 7,745 workers as a single agency: its proprietor, Mohammad Ali Sarkar, said many who had demand from Malaysia sent workers through it, that it took written undertakings from those agencies on responsibility for the workers, and that it took only the government-fixed amount <sup>[N75 (2024)](#n75)</sup>.
- **Five M International:** agencies inside the syndicate blamed the workers' extra cost on agencies outside it, which they said bought demand letters from Malaysia at high prices in unhealthy competition; Five M's managing director, Masud Uddin Chowdhury, said on 30 May 2024 that he had not bought even 100 jobs, that all came automatically through the software, that those whose jobs came sent workers through Five M, and that it only did visa processing and took only the government-fixed fee <sup>[N75 (2024)](#n75)</sup>.

**2026: the same position, agency owners say.** The four agency owners told Prothom Alo in September 2026 that an associate will be in the same position, since it cannot send workers directly, and that listed agencies approved without experience are unknown to Malaysian employers, so they will send workers through experienced agencies and take the syndicate fee in return <sup>[N76](#n76)</sup>. Riazul Islam, BAIRA's immediate past senior vice-president, said no agency applied to be an associate, they were simply made associates; that jobs will come in the names of the 25, not of the associates; and that at least 817 agencies sent workers in the earlier round by paying the syndicate fee <sup>[N76](#n76)</sup>. BAIRA members said about 1,400 Bangladeshi agencies were once listed on FWCMS, but only 10 were approved in 2016, and in 2022, 25 at first and finally 100 <sup>[N76](#n76)</sup>. In June 2026, The Edge, a Malaysian business paper, reported (as carried by Prothom Alo) that 102 Bangladeshi firms were then approved under FWCMS and that Bangladesh was considering including 432 recruiting agencies instead; a manpower-sector executive told it that Bangladesh wanted recruitment opened to more Bangladeshi companies rather than a few chosen by some influential people <sup>[N54](#n54)</sup>.

> **Need to know**
> - How many Bangladeshi agencies held FWCMS approval before the 2026 list: 100 <sup>[N76](#n76)</sup> or 102 <sup>[N54](#n54)</sup>?
> - Was the final list the interim government sent Malaysia 423 agencies <sup>[N76](#n76)</sup> or 432 <sup>[N54](#n54)</sup>?

<a id="how-the-toll-was-collected"></a>

#### 1.3.7 How the toll was collected

A Prothom Alo investigation published on 17 September 2024 set out how the 2022-24 toll was collected, from agency owners, syndicate members, company records and Ministry letters <sup>[N153 (2024)](#n153)</sup>.

**The e-wallet.** Every worker going to Malaysia had to be registered in Migram, the software formally called FWCMS, whose registration fee was RM100 (about Tk 2,700); agency owners said about Tk 1,07,000 was taken per worker instead, of which the local collecting firm kept Tk 7,000 and sent Tk 1 lakh to Bestinet in Malaysia, the transactions being illegal <sup>[N153 (2024)](#n153)</sup>. Only syndicate members' names were in Migram, and they paid the RM100 per worker through an e-wallet; two agency owners said Bestinet let the fee be paid from the e-wallet only after Tk 1,07,000 had been deposited for that worker <sup>[N153 (2024)](#n153)</sup>. On 9 May 2023 the Ministry had written to the High Commission in Kuala Lumpur asking it to take up with Malaysia complaints from several agencies that Bestinet was not allocating e-wallet top-ups, a problem several Malaysian employers had also reported to Malaysia's home ministry <sup>[N153 (2024)](#n153)</sup>.

**The cash.** The money had to be deposited at the Madani Avenue, Dhaka, office of Catharsis International, the agency of Ruhul Amin (Swapan), whose staff took in crores of taka in cash every day, two syndicate members said <sup>[N153 (2024)](#n153)</sup>. S M Rafiq, managing director of United Export Limited, itself a syndicate member, said every syndicate agency had to deliver Tk 1,07,000 a worker to the Catharsis office <sup>[N153 (2024)](#n153)</sup>.

**The company records.** Records of the RJSC (Registrar of Joint Stock Companies and Firms, Bangladesh's company registry) show Bestinet registered in Bangladesh on 24 March 2014 as Bestinet Bangladesh Limited, with Aminul Islam bin Amin Nur as chairman and Mohammad Ruhul Amin as managing director <sup>[N153 (2024)](#n153)</sup>. Prothom Alo also reported that Amin held 10,000 of its 12,000 shares and Ruhul Amin 2,000, that on 28 March 2019 Amin's shares were transferred to Ruhul Amin and his brother Saiful Islam became a director, and that in 2022 the company was renamed Catharsis Solutions Limited <sup>[N153 (2024)](#n153)</sup>. Ruhul Amin told Prothom Alo that Bestinet Bangladesh had no connection with Malaysia's Bestinet and that Amin gave up his share because he got no business in Bangladesh; on 20 June 2024 he called the allegations of taking and laundering money false, and said both governments gave all approvals and agencies with good contacts in Malaysia got approval after heavy lobbying <sup>[N153 (2024)](#n153)</sup>.

**The visa office.** On 2 January 2023, acting on a BAIRA letter, the Ministry wrote to the High Commission that most agencies selected through FWCMS lacked the facilities and capacity to recruit and send workers, that automatic selection let them charge high migration costs, and that although 12 visa centres were approved, only FWCMS's Dhaka office, MEFC (Malaysia Employment Facilitation Centre), issued workers' e-visas; it asked for visas to be issued from several centres <sup>[N153 (2024)](#n153)</sup>. MEFC's office is in Catharsis Tower in Banani, Dhaka; Malaysia-bound workers' passports were submitted there for the visa and agencies collected them there, sources told Prothom Alo <sup>[N153 (2024)](#n153)</sup>.

<a id="who-controls-it"></a>

#### 1.3.8 Who controls it

**Bestinet and Amin.** Bestinet was founded by Aminul Islam Abdul Nor ("Amin"), a Bangladesh-born Malaysian; Malaysia's Auditor-General found it collected RM100 per worker without a formal government contract <sup>[23](#o23), [N158](#n158), [N159 (2023)](#n159)</sup>. Bestinet owns Migram, the software of FWCMS; the Bangla press calls Amin "Amin Nor" or "Amin Nur" <sup>[N75 (2024)](#n75), [N76](#n76)</sup>. People in the sector told Prothom Alo in June 2024 that Bestinet's contract with the Malaysian government ended on 31 May 2024, that Malaysia stopped taking workers so as not to renew it with this controversial company, but that the contract might now be extended by three years <sup>[N160 (2024)](#n160)</sup>. Bestinet is supposed to receive RM100 (Tk 2,500) per worker, but is alleged to have done illegal business by controlling the whole process in the name of selecting agencies, Prothom Alo reported in April 2025 <sup>[N29](#n29)</sup>. In October 2025 it reported that Amin had stepped down as Bestinet's chairman but still holds a stake, that at least Tk 8,750 crore is alleged to have been laundered out of Bangladesh by a ring in the Malaysia labour market under the Awami League government, and that manpower exporters claim Amin and his Bangladeshi agent Ruhul Amin led it <sup>[N161](#n161)</sup>. Asked on 20 August 2025 about Amin Nur, the expatriates' welfare adviser, Asif Nazrul, said he works with the Malaysian government, that Bangladesh has nothing to discuss with him, and that talks are held directly with Malaysia's government <sup>[N129](#n129)</sup>.

**Amin and Ruhul Amin Swapan, owner of Catharsis International.** The Daily Star reports that the two led the 2022-24 syndicate; Bangladesh police sought their extradition in 2024, both deny wrongdoing, and Amin has not been charged <sup>[N162](#n162), [N163](#n163), [N102 (2024)](#n102), [N158](#n158)</sup>. Prothom Alo reported in May 2024 the allegation that Amin controls the whole worker-sending trade from Malaysia, that Swapan, a former BAIRA secretary general, acts as his representative in Bangladesh, and that the two decided who would be in the syndicate <sup>[N75 (2024)](#n75)</sup>. BAIRA members allege that Swapan led the forming of the 2022 syndicate, fixed the names of 69 of its 100 agencies and was the channel for money transacted in Malaysia; he did not respond to Prothom Alo <sup>[N147 (2024)](#n147)</sup>. RMMRU, a migration research body, said in its report "Trends in labour migration from Bangladesh 2024", presented on 28 December 2024, that Amin, owner of Malaysia's foreign-worker recruitment software, and Swapan jointly built the syndicate, which sent five and a half lakh workers and embezzled USD 200 crore (Tk 24,000 crore) from migrants <sup>[N164 (2024)](#n164)</sup>. Prothom Alo reported in September 2026 that Swapan has been in hiding abroad since the fall of the Awami League government in the 2024 uprising, that his agency's licence has been withdrawn, a case against him is under way, and the ACC is also investigating <sup>[N76](#n76)</sup>.

**Bringing them back.** Malaysia's Home Ministry confirmed in November 2024 that it had received Bangladesh's arrest request, which accused Amin and Ruhul Amin of money laundering, extortion and trafficking foreign workers; the minister said then that Malaysia wanted Dhaka to clarify whether the purpose was extradition or further investigation <sup>[N161](#n161)</sup>. Malaysia's Home Minister, Saifuddin Nasution Ismail, said in October 2025 that the two countries' police were working on Bangladesh's request to bring the two back, through government-to-government and police-to-police coordination agreed with Chief Adviser Muhammad Yunus during his visit <sup>[N161](#n161)</sup>. In June 2026 the matter was still under discussion between the two governments, The Edge reported <sup>[N54](#n54)</sup>.

<a id="how-the-2022-24-syndicate-was-formed"></a>

#### 1.3.9 How the 2022-24 syndicate was formed

When Malaysia reopened in July 2022 after nearly four years, the choice of agencies was left to the Malaysian government; the Ministry sent it a list of 1,520 recruiting agencies and only 25 were chosen <sup>[N147 (2024)](#n147), [N165](#n165)</sup>. Prothom Alo reported that there was no policy for the selection and many allegations of influence and bribery in it <sup>[N147 (2024)](#n147)</sup>.
